Tensor structures on fibered categories
Luca Terenzi

Abstract
. Let 𝒮 \mathscr{S} be a small category admitting binary products. We show that the whole theory of monoidal 𝒮 \mathscr{S} -fibered categories, which is customarily formulated in terms of the usual internal tensor product, can be rephrased purely in terms of the associated external tensor product. More precisely, we construct a canonical dictionary relating the classical structures and properties of the internal tensor product to analogous structures and properties of the external tensor product; the translation between the internal version and the external version of the theory is mediated by suitable notions of equivalence. Our method applies to associativity, commutativity, and unit constraints, to projection formulae, as well as to monoidality of morphisms between monoidal 𝒮 \mathscr{S} -fibered categories. For instance, we show how Mac Lane’s classical pentagon and hexagon axioms can be stated using the external tensor product. Our results provide a satisfactory abstract framework to study monoidal structures in the setting of perverse sheaves.



†
†

1991
Mathematics Subject Classification
. 18D30, 18M05.
†
†

Key words and phrases
. Monoidal fibered categories, external tensor product, perverse sheaves.

Introduction

Motivation and goal of the paper

Given a small category
\mathscr{S}
, the theory of
\mathscr{S}
-fibered categories can be merged with the theory of monoidal categories into a theory of
monoidal
\mathscr{S}
-fibered categories
. The basic definitions of this theory can be found, for example, in
[
Ayo07a
, § 2.3.1]
, where fibered categories are regarded as contravariant pseudo-functors with domain
\mathscr{S}
rather than as fibrations over
\mathscr{S}
; the same perspective is adopted throughout the present paper. In a nutshell, giving a monoidal
\mathscr{S}
-fibered category amounts to giving an
\mathscr{S}
-fibered category
\mathbb{H}
equipped with a collection of tensor product functors

-\otimes-=-\otimes_{S}-:\mathbb{H}(S)\times\mathbb{H}(S)\rightarrow\mathbb{H}(S),\quad(A,B)\rightsquigarrow A\otimes B\qquad(S\in\mathscr{S})

carrying an associativity constraint (and possibly also a commutativity constraint and a unit constraint) together with, for every morphism
f:T\rightarrow S
in
\mathscr{S}
, a natural isomorphism of functors
\mathbb{H}(S)\times\mathbb{H}(S)\rightarrow\mathbb{H}(T)

(1)

m=m_{f}:f^{*}A\otimes f^{*}B\xrightarrow{\sim}f^{*}(A\otimes B)

witnessing the monoidality of the inverse image functor
f^{*}:\mathbb{H}(S)\rightarrow\mathbb{H}(T)
. These structures are required to satisfy a certain list of compatibility conditions ensuring that any two given iterates of tensor product and inverse image functors do not get identified in more than one way.
If the base category
\mathscr{S}
admits binary products, one can also define the
external tensor product
functors

-\boxtimes-=-\boxtimes_{S_{1},S_{2}}-:\mathbb{H}(S_{1})\times\mathbb{H}(S_{2})\rightarrow\mathbb{H}(S_{1}\times S_{2}),\quad(A_{1},A_{2})\mapsto A_{1}\boxtimes A_{2}\qquad(S_{1},S_{2}\in\mathscr{S})

by the formula

(2)

A_{1}\boxtimes A_{2}:=pr^{12*}_{1}A_{1}\otimes pr^{12*}_{2}A_{2},

where
pr^{12}_{i}:S_{1}\times S_{2}\rightarrow S_{i}
,
i=1,2
, denote the canonical projections. Informally, the usual tensor product - also called the
internal tensor product
- can be recovered from the external tensor product via the formula

(3)

A\otimes B=\Delta_{S}^{*}(A\boxtimes B),

where
\Delta_{S}:S\hookrightarrow S\times S
denotes the diagonal embedding.
However, strictly speaking, the two constructions in (
2
) and (
3
) are not mutually inverse. Moreover, it is a priori unclear whether the monoidality isomorphisms (
1
) and the various additional constraints (together with their coherence and mutual compatibility conditions) can be expressed purely in terms of the external tensor product. A similar issue arises when studying monoidality of morphisms between
\mathscr{S}
-fibered categories equipped with monoidal structures. The main goal of this paper is to provide a precise answer to all these questions.

Our main motivation for this abstract work comes from the theory of perverse sheaves over algebraic or analytic varieties. To fix notation, let
k
be a subfield of the complex numbers, and consider the category
\mathsf{Var}_{k}
of (quasi-projective) algebraic
k
-varieties. To every
S\in\mathsf{Var}_{k}
one can attach the algebraically constructible
\mathbb{Q}
-linear derived category
D^{b}_{c}(S,\mathbb{Q})
, a tensor-triangulated category; as
S
varies, these categories assemble into a monoidal
\mathsf{Var}_{k}
-fibered category. The theory of
[
BBD82
]
yields a perverse
t
-structure on
D^{b}_{c}(S,\mathbb{Q})
whose heart is the
\mathbb{Q}
-linear abelian category of perverse sheaves on
S
, denoted
\mathsf{Perv}(S)
. As shown by Beilinson in his celebrated paper
[
Bei87
]
, the triangulated category
D^{b}_{c}(S,\mathbb{Q})
can be canonically recovered as the bounded derived category
D^{b}(\mathsf{Perv}(S))
. In light of Beilinson’s result, it is natural to wonder how much of the functoriality of the triangulated categories
D^{b}_{c}(S,\mathbb{Q})
can be reconstructed directly on the level of perverse shaves.

This question is not a mere formal quirk.
Over the last decades, the categories
D^{b}_{c}(S,\mathbb{Q})
have served as a model for several other refined triangulated systems of coefficient over algebraic varieties. In some cases, it has proven successful to construct the triangulated coefficient categories directly as bounded derived categories of suitable abelian categories of enhanced perverse sheaves. The most remarkable example is M. Saito’s theory of
mixed Hodge modules
, developed in
[
Sai90
]
, which provides a system of coefficients enhancing the classical theory of mixed Hodge structures. Saito’s triangulated categories enjoy a rich functoriality which perfectly mimics that of the underlying constructible derived categories; in particular, they assemble into a monoidal
\mathsf{Var}_{k}
-fibered category. Saito’s approach to the tensor structure on mixed Hodge modules uses the external perspective in an essential way: it is the internal tensor product that is defined from the external one via the above-mentioned formula (
3
), not the other way around. The fundamental reason behind Saito’s approach is the lack of perverse
t
-exactness of the internal tensor product: the bi-triangulated functor

-\otimes-:D^{b}_{c}(S,\mathbb{Q})\times D^{b}_{c}(S,\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow D^{b}_{c}(S,\mathbb{Q})

does not respect the perverse
t
-structure unless
\dim(S)=0
. On the other hand, the external tensor product

-\boxtimes-:D^{b}_{c}(S_{1},\mathbb{Q})\times D^{b}_{c}(S_{2},\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow D^{b}_{c}(S_{1}\times S_{2},\mathbb{Q})

does always respect the perverse
t
-structures; it restricts to a bi-exact functor

-\boxtimes-:\mathsf{Perv}(S_{1})\times\mathsf{Perv}(S_{2})\rightarrow\mathsf{Perv}(S_{1}\times S_{2})

and, in fact, it can be canonically recovered from the latter modulo Beilinson’s equivalences. In the setting of mixed Hodge modules, one is thus led to construct the external tensor product on the perverse hearts in the first place. (Note that what ultimately prevents the internal tensor product from being
t
-exact is the lack of
t
-exactness of the inverse image functor
\Delta_{S}^{*}
; however, it turns out that the latter can be conveniently expressed in terms of
t
-exact functors, which saves the day. We prefer to gloss over this point, as it will play no role in the present paper.) It is fair to say that Saito’s impressive work deals mainly with the concrete definition of the single functors on mixed Hodge modules, leaving the most genuinely category-theoretic aspects of the construction aside. In particular, the associativity, symmetry, and unitarity properties of Saito’s monoidal structure are not studied systematically in
[
Sai90
]
. Our work thus provides an adequate abstract framework for Saito’s construction. Based on the same general framework, in
[
Ter24F
]
and
[
Ter24E
]
we further investigate more specific questions related to the monoidality of inverse image functors and the construction of unit constraints.

The same need for precise foundations for the theory of monoidal fibered categories based on the external tensor product is even more visible in the setting of
perverse Nori motives
, a further motivic refinement of Saito’s coefficient system recently introduced by F. Ivorra and S. Morel in
[
IM24
]
.
Again, the starting datum is a system of abelian categories of enhanced perverse sheaves, and the goal is to equip their bounded derived categories with the same functoriality as the underlying constructible derived categories.
Contrary to the case of mixed Hodge modules, perverse Nori motives are defined by a purely categorical construction, which prevents one from describing their objects and morphisms in any concrete manner.
In fact, the nature of Ivorra and Morel’s categories makes it impossible to construct functors on them if not by lifting suitable
t
-exact functors on the constructible derived categories, as an abstract application of a certain categorical universal property.
As long as one is concerned with the definition of the single functors, Saito’s ideas can be successfully adapted to the motivic setting.
However, making sure that these functors give rise to a coherent structure is a rather delicate question, for the structural reasons just mentioned.
In our article
[
Ter24N
]
, we apply the general method developed here to constructing the monoidal structure on perverse Nori motives, again starting from the external tensor product on the perverse hearts. In the motivic setting, the highly inexplicit nature of the categories involved forces us to be as formally precise as possible. For this reason, having a general dictionary expressing the usual structures and properties of the internal tensor product as structures and properties of the external tensor product is highly desirable in our opinion.

Main results

In order not to restrict the application range of our techniques unnecessarily, and also for sake of notational and conceptual clarity, we formulate our results in the general framework of fibered categories, thus avoiding any reference to perverse sheaves or to concrete geometric situations.

Given a small category
\mathscr{S}
admitting binary products, we introduce the language of
internal and external tensor structures
on a given
\mathscr{S}
-fibered category
\mathbb{H}
.
Our treatment of internal tensor structures is just a re-arrangement of the classical theory of monoidal
\mathscr{S}
-fibered categories: for example, we introduce associativity, commutativity, and unit constraints (as well as their compatibility conditions) as formalized in Mac Lane’s classical paper
[
Mac63
]
, and we describe their compatibility with inverse image functors as done in Ayoub’s work
[
Ayo07a
]
. The only difference is that, in order to make the exposition as neat as possible, we prefer not to include an associativity constraint from the very beginning but rather treat it as an accessory structure, exactly as one sometimes does with commutativity and unit constraints.
Our theory of external tensor structures should be new in the literature. The main notions that we introduce are the natural transliteration of the corresponding notions for internal tensor structures. This operation works nicely both for monoidality isomorphisms and for each of the three types of additional constraint (as well as for their compatibility conditions).

Motivated by the theory of perverse sheaves and by the abstract theory of six functor formalisms (as formalized in
[
Ayo07a
,
Ayo07b
]
,
[
CD19
]
or
[
DG22
]
), we also study the so-called
projection formulae
in our setting of tensor structures. Not surprisingly, it turns out that the usual formulation in terms of the internal tensor product admits a natural translation in terms of the external tensor product.
Again, the main difference with respect to the existing literature is that we treat the validity of projection formulae as a separate property rather than imposing it from the beginning.

Generally speaking, an important ingredient in our presentation is the notion of
equivalence
between internal or external tensor structures: this allows us to make it precise in which sense the classical formulae (
2
) and (
3
) should be considered to be mutually inverse.
Our first main result can be stated as follows:

Theorem

(
6.1
,
7.7
)
.

Fix an
\mathscr{S}
-fibered category
\mathbb{H}
. Then there exist canonical mutually inverse bijections between equivalence classes of (unitary, symmetric, associative) internal and external tensor structures on
\mathbb{H}
. Moreover, these bijections respect the validity of projection formulae.

It is worth noting that all the equivalences between tensor structures involved in the proofs are explicit and canonical, so that the actual result is more precise than what asserted.

In the same spirit, given a morphism of
\mathscr{S}
-fibered categories
R:\mathbb{H}_{1}\rightarrow\mathbb{H}_{2}
, we introduce the notion of
internal and external tensor structures on
R
(with respect to two given internal or external tensor structures on
\mathbb{H}_{1}
and
\mathbb{H}_{2}
, respectively). In the internal setting, we recover the usual notion of monoidal morphism between monoidal
\mathscr{S}
-fibered categories; in the external setting, we consider its natural transliteration. We then study the compatibility of internal and external tensor structures on
R
with given associativity, commutativity, or unit constraints on
\mathbb{H}_{1}
and
\mathbb{H}_{2}
.
Our second main result can be stated as follows:

Theorem

(
8.1
)
.

Fix a morphism of
\mathscr{S}
-fibered categories
R:\mathbb{H}_{1}\rightarrow\mathbb{H}_{2}
. Then, under the bijective correspondence of the previous theorem, there exist canonical mutually inverse bijections between (unitary, symmetric, associative) internal and external tensor structures on
R
with respect to two given (unitary, symmetric, associative) tensor structures on
\mathbb{H}_{1}
and
\mathbb{H}_{2}
.

In order for this statement to even make sense, one needs to use the explicit equivalences witnessing the bijections between internal and external tensor structures on
\mathbb{H}_{1}
and on
\mathbb{H}_{2}
as mentioned above.

Concretely, all the axioms introduced in the course of the paper ask for the commutativity of suitable natural diagrams; similarly, both the main results described above and all the intermediate results used to prove them amount to showing the commutativity of certain natural diagrams. Checking the commutativity of these diagrams directly is not hard: in fact, it does not require any new insight once the various claims are made explicit. The main issue is that one often has to decompose the diagrams under consideration into several pieces, and the resulting figures rarely fit into the space of one or two pages.
As a partial workaround, we have chosen to include a few auxiliary results (
2.4
,
2.8
,
8.3
and
8.6
) which allow us to cleverly simplify the diagrams in question; in this way, it also becomes easier to understand the roles that the various axioms play in each proof.

These auxiliary results have independent interest, since they make it clear that internal and external tensor structures, both on fibered categories and on morphisms of such, are just suitable variations on the theme of fibered categories.
For instance, we show that an internal tensor structure on a given
\mathscr{S}
-fibered category can be encoded into a plain fibered category over
\mathscr{S}\times\mathscr{I}
, where
\mathscr{I}
denotes the category with two objects and one non-identity arrow (
2.5
); similarly, an external tensor structure can be encoded into a plain fibered category over
\mathscr{S}^{2}\times\mathscr{I}
. One could certainly push this point of view further so as to include associativity, commutativity, and unit constraints; we leave this task to the interested reader.

Related work

The current literature counts several papers devoted to the foundations of monoidal fibered categories at various levels of abstraction.
The interpretation of monoidal structures on
\mathscr{S}
-fibered categories in terms of categories fibered over a lager base discussed the present paper was influenced by F. Hörmann’s approach to six functor formalisms in the language of derivators, as developed in
[
Hör17
]
.
The classical Grothendieck construction relating contravariant pseudo-functors with domain
\mathscr{S}
to fibrations over
\mathscr{S}
admits a monoidal analogue, investigated in
[
Shu08
]
and
[
MV20
]
in the 2-categorical setting and in
[
Ram22
]
in the
\infty
-categorical setting.

The present paper differs in spirit from these and similar works: our aim is not to encode the whole theory of monoidal fibered categories into a synthetic conceptual structure, but rather to turn the usual set of explicit axioms of the theory (given in terms of the internal tensor product) into a different but equivalent explicit set of axioms (tailored to the external tensor product).
This means that our results can be used in practice to construct well-behaved monoidal structures on fibered categories in those contexts where it is more natural to work with the external than with the internal tensor product: one can construct associativity, commutativity, and unit constraints directly in terms of the external tensor product, and the coherence of the monoidal structure is guaranteed by an explicit list of natural conditions which often can be checked by hand.
We believe that this is the main advantage offered by our approach.

Structure of the paper

Throughout the paper, we work over a fixed small category
\mathscr{S}
. Essentially, all fibered categories involved are fibered over
\mathscr{S}
; the only exception is represented by certain auxiliary fibered categories over larger bases (such as
\mathscr{S}\times\mathscr{I}
or
\mathscr{S}^{2}\times\mathscr{I}
, as already mentioned) which are not involved explicitly in the proofs of the main results.

In
Section

1
we collect the general notions on fibered categories employed in the paper, including those of section, morphism, and image of a section under a morphism. We also prove a couple of useful auxiliary results (
1.3
and
1.8
) which are repeatedly used in the sequel in order to shorten our constructions and simplify certain proofs.

Starting from
Section

2
, we assume that the base category
\mathscr{S}
admits binary products: this is only needed to define the external tensor product; it is irrelevant for our discussion whether
\mathscr{S}
possesses or not a terminal object. In
Section

2
we explain how to rephrase the structure of a monoidal
\mathscr{S}
-fibered category purely in terms of the external tensor product. In order to highlight the symmetry between the internal and external versions of the tensor product, it is useful to write down the corresponding axioms of the two versions of the theory in parallel. To this end, we introduce appropriate notions of
internal tensor structure
and of
external tensor structure
(
2.2
(1) and
2.6
(1), respectively) that only include the single tensor product functors and their monoidality isomorphisms. We then explain how to transform internal tensor structures into external tensor structures (
2.10
) and conversely (
2.11
). Moreover, we introduce natural notions of
equivalence
between internal or external tensor structures (
2.2
(2) and
2.6
(2), respectively) that allow us to make it precise in what sense the two formulae (
2
) and (
3
) can be promoted to mutually inverse constructions (
2.12
).

In
Section

3
,
Section

4
and
Section

5
we consider associativity, commutativity, and unit constraints, respectively. Following the approach of
Section

2
, we start by introducing the internal and external versions of the relevant constraint, and we refine the notions of equivalence of tensor structures accordingly; we then explain how to transform an internal constraint into an external one and conversely; lastly, we show that the canonical bijection between equivalence classes of internal and external tensor structures can be refined compatibly with the constraints under consideration.

In
Section

6
we examine the mutual compatibility conditions between associativity, commutativity, and unit constraints. Again, each of these condition can be formulated both in the internal and in the external setting, and the homologous conditions correspond to one another under the bijection between internal and external tensor structures. Once we have this last ingredient, we are able to deduce our first main result (
6.1
) in its full strength.

The short
Section

7
is devoted to the study of projection formulae, which are an important property of most monoidal fibered categories of geometric nature. We show that the usual projection formulae in the language of internal tensor structures have a natural analogue in the language of external tensor structures in such a way that the bijection of
2.12
respects the validity of projection formulae (
7.7
).

In
Section

8
we study monoidality of morphisms between monoidal
\mathscr{S}
-fibered categories. In the first half of the section, we introduce the notion of
internal tensor structure
and of
external tensor structure
on a morphism of
\mathscr{S}
-fibered categories with respect to given internal or external tensor structures on the two single
\mathscr{S}
-fibered categories involved (
8.2
and
8.5
, respectively). By analogy with the case of monoidal structures on single
\mathscr{S}
-fibered categories, we explain how to transform internal tensor structures on a morphism into external tensor structures on the same morphism (
8.9
) and conversely (
8.10
); this is done coherently with the translation procedure between internal and external tensor structures on the two
\mathscr{S}
-fibered categories involved. Then we prove that these two constructions are mutually inverse modulo equivalence of internal and external tensor structures on the two sides (
8.11
).
In the second half of the section, we refine such a bijection with respect to associativity, commutativity, and unit constraints: to this end, we consider the natural properties of associativity, symmetry and unitarity for tensor structures on morphisms, both in the internal and in the external setting. We show without difficulty that the correspondence between internal and external tensor structures is compatible with each of these properties. Once we have this last ingredient, we are able to deduce our second main result (
8.1
) in its full strength.

In the final
Section

9
, motivated by our applications to the setting of perverse sheaves, we specialize our discussion to the case of triangulated
\mathscr{S}
-fibered categories. We fully spell out the natural triangulated analogues of the various definitions and results obtained previously, but we omit all details of proof. The commutativity conventions that we have chosen here are coherent with the usual conventions for derived categories of abelian (
\mathscr{S}
-fibered) categories endowed with bi-exact tensor product functors.

Notation and conventions

•

Unless otherwise dictated, categories are assumed to be small with respect to some fixed universe.

•

Let
\mathscr{S}
be a small category.

–

The notation
S\in\mathscr{S}
means that
S
is an object of
\mathscr{S}
.

–

A
composable tuple
in
\mathscr{S}
is a tuple of the form
\underline{g}=(g_{1},\dots,g_{r})
where
g_{1},\dots,g_{r}
are morphisms in
\mathscr{S}
such that the composition
g_{r}\circ\cdots\circ g_{1}
makes sense in
\mathscr{S}
.

–

We say that a sequence
\underline{g}^{(1)}=(g_{1}^{(1)},\dots,g_{r_{1}}^{(1)}),\dots,\underline{g}^{(n)}=(g_{1}^{(n)},\dots,g_{r_{n}}^{(n)})
of composable tuples in
\mathscr{S}
is
composable
if, for each
i=1,\dots,n-1
, the composition
g_{1}^{(i+1)}\circ g_{r_{i}}^{(i)}
makes sense in
\mathscr{S}
.

•

Let
\mathscr{S}
be a small category admitting binary products.

–

Given two composable tuples in
\mathscr{S}
with the same length

\underline{g}^{(1)}=(g_{1}^{(1)},\dots,g_{r}^{(1)}),\quad\underline{g}^{(2)}=(g_{1}^{(2)},\dots,g_{r}^{(2)}),

we let
\underline{g}^{(1)}\times\underline{g}^{(2)}
denote the composable tuple
(g_{1}^{(1)}\times g_{1}^{(2)},\dots,g_{r}^{(1)}\times g_{r}^{(2)})
.

–

For every object
S\in\mathscr{S}
and every integer
n\geq 2
, we let

\Delta_{S}^{(n)}:S\hookrightarrow S\times\dots\times S

denote the diagonal embedding of
S
into the direct product of
n
copies of
S
in
\mathscr{S}
. In the special case where
n=2
, we omit the superscript from the notation.

–

Given a collection of objects
S_{i_{1}},\dots,S_{i_{n}}\in\mathscr{S}
indexed by natural numbers
i_{1},\dots,i_{n}
, for every substring
j_{1}\dots j_{r}
of the string
i_{1}\dots i_{n}
we let

pr^{i_{1}\dots i_{n}}_{j_{1}\dots j_{r}}:S_{i_{1}}\times\dots\times S_{i_{n}}\rightarrow S_{j_{1}}\times\dots\times S_{j_{r}}

denote the canonical projection.

–

Given a collection of objects
S_{i_{1}},\dots,S_{i_{n}}\in\mathscr{S}
indexed by a set of natural numbers
I=\left\{i_{1},\dots,i_{n}\right\}
, for every permutation
\sigma\in\textup{Sym}(I)
we let

\tau_{\sigma}:S_{i_{1}}\times\dots S_{i_{n}}\xrightarrow{\sim}S_{\sigma(i_{1})}\times\dots\times S_{\sigma(i_{n})}

denote the canonical permutation isomorphism. In the special case where
n=2
and
\sigma
is the non-identity permutation, we omit the subscript from the notation.

•

Given categories
\mathscr{C}_{1},\dots,\mathscr{C}_{n}
, we let
\mathscr{C}_{1}\times\cdots\times\mathscr{C}_{n}
denote their direct product category.

1.
Recollections on fibered categories

Throughout this paper, we fix a small category
\mathscr{S}
; starting from
Section

2
we will need to assume binary products to exist in
\mathscr{S}
, but this is not necessary for the moment. All fibered categories mentioned in this paper are fibered over
\mathscr{S}
unless stated otherwise.

The conventions on
2
-categories and
2
-functors that we follow are those adopted in
[
Ayo07a
, § 1.4.1]
and in
[
CD19
, § 1.1]
: what we call a fibered category here is what in the original reference
[
SGA1
, Exp. VI, Defn. 7.1]
is called a "catégorie clivée normalisée"; we consider
2
-categories in the strict sense but
2
-functors in the lax sense. See
[
Mac71
, § XII.3]
for generalities on
2
-categories.

1.1.
Fibered categories and sections

In the first part of this section, we quickly review
\mathscr{S}
-fibered categories. We define an
\mathscr{S}
-fibered category as a contravariant
2
-functor from
\mathscr{S}
to the
2
-category of small categories. For simplicity, we assume every such
2
-functor to be strictly unital; in fact, this is not a severe requirement (see for example
[
Del01
, Lemma 2.5]
). In detail:

Definition 1.1
.

An
\mathscr{S}
-fibered category

\mathbb{H}
is the datum of

•

for every
S\in\mathscr{S}
, a category
\mathbb{H}(S)
,

•

for every morphism
f:T\rightarrow S
in
\mathscr{S}
, a functor

f^{*}:\mathbb{H}(S)\rightarrow\mathbb{H}(T),

called the
inverse image functor
along
f
,

•

for every pair of composable morphisms
f:T\rightarrow S
and
g:S\rightarrow V
in
\mathscr{S}
, a natural isomorphism of functors
\mathbb{H}(V)\rightarrow\mathbb{H}(T)

(4)

\mathsf{conn}=\mathsf{conn}_{f,g}:(gf)^{*}A\xrightarrow{\sim}f^{*}g^{*}A

called the
connection isomorphism
at
(f,g)

such that the following conditions are satisfied:

(
\mathscr{S}
-fib-0)

For every
S\in\mathscr{S}
, we have
\textup{id}_{S}^{*}=\textup{id}_{\mathbb{H}(S)}
.

(
\mathscr{S}
-fib-1)

For every triple of composable morphisms
f:T\rightarrow S
,
g:S\rightarrow V
and
h:V\rightarrow W
in
\mathscr{S}
, the diagram of functors
\mathbb{H}(W)\rightarrow\mathbb{H}(T)

(5)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(hgf)^{*}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}(hg)^{*}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(gf)^{*}h^{*}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}g^{*}h^{*}A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{conn}_{f,hg}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{conn}_{gf,h}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{conn}_{g,h}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{conn}_{f,g}}

is commutative.

Notation 1.2
.

Given a composable tuple
\underline{g}=(g_{1},\dots,g_{r})
in
\mathscr{S}
, we let
\underline{g}^{*}
denote the composite of inverse image functors
g_{1}^{*}\circ\cdots\circ g_{r}^{*}
in any
\mathscr{S}
-fibered category.
In order to avoid possible confusion, we agree that giving an empty composable tuple
\underline{g}
just means giving an object
S\in\mathscr{S}
; in this case, we set
\underline{g}^{*}:=\textup{id}_{S}^{*}=\textup{id}_{\mathbb{H}(S)}
.

The following result explains the full meaning of axiom (
\mathscr{S}
-fib-1); it will serve as a model for similar results obtained in
Section

2
and
Section

8
.

Lemma 1.3
.

Let
\mathbb{H}
be a
\mathscr{S}
-fibered category, and fix a morphism
f:T\rightarrow S
in
\mathscr{S}
. For every composable tuple in
\mathscr{S}

\underline{g}=(g_{1},\dots,g_{r})

satisfying
g_{r}\circ\cdots\circ g_{1}=f
, consider the functor
F_{\underline{g}}:\mathbb{H}(S)\rightarrow\mathbb{H}(T)
defined by the formula

F_{\underline{g}}(A):=\underline{g}^{*}A.

Then, given any two tuples
\underline{g}^{(1)}
,
\underline{g}^{(2)}
as above, all natural isomorphisms of functors
\mathbb{H}(S)\rightarrow\mathbb{H}(T)

F_{\underline{g}^{(1)}}(A)\xrightarrow{\sim}F_{\underline{g}^{(2)}}(A)

obtained by composing connection isomorphisms (and inverses thereof) coincide.

Proof.

It suffices to show that, for every tuple
\underline{g}
as in the statement, every natural isomorphism of functors
\mathbb{H}(S)\rightarrow\mathbb{H}(T)

\phi:F_{\underline{g}}(A)\xrightarrow{\sim}f^{*}A

obtained by composing connection isomorphisms (and inverses thereof) coincides with the composite of inverse connection isomorphisms

\alpha_{\underline{g}}:F_{\underline{g}}(A):=g_{1}^{*}\cdots g_{r}^{*}A\xleftarrow{\mathsf{conn}}(g_{2}g_{1})^{*}\cdots g_{r}^{*}\xleftarrow{\mathsf{conn}}\dots\xleftarrow{\mathsf{conn}}(g_{r}\dots g_{1})^{*}A=f^{*}A.

To see this, write
\phi
explicitly as

\phi:F_{\underline{g}^{(0)}}\xrightarrow{\phi_{1}}F_{\underline{g}^{(1)}}\xrightarrow{\phi_{2}}\dots\xrightarrow{\phi_{N}}F_{\underline{g}^{(N)}}

where we set
\underline{g}^{(0)}:=\underline{g}
,
\underline{g}^{(N)}:=(f)
, and each factor
\phi_{i}
is either a direct or an inverse connection isomorphism; note that, by construction, the number of inverse minus the number of direct connection isomorphisms among the
\phi_{i}
’s equals
r-1
. We prove our claim by analyzing the following cases separately:

•

Suppose that all factors
\phi_{i}
are inverse connection isomorphisms. Then, by repeated applications of axiom (
\mathscr{S}
-fib-1), the claim follows by induction on
N=r-1
.

•

Suppose that there exists an index
n
with
0<n<N
such that
\phi_{i}
is a direct connection isomorphism for
1\leq i\leq n
and an inverse connection isomorphism for
n+1\leq i\leq N
. Then we can consider the factorization

\phi:F_{\underline{g}^{(0)}}\xrightarrow{\psi_{1}}F_{\underline{g}^{(n)}}\xrightarrow{\psi_{2}}F_{\underline{g}^{(N)}}

where
\psi_{1}:=\phi_{n}\circ\dots\circ\phi_{1}
and
\psi_{2}:=\phi_{N}\circ\dots\circ\phi_{n+1}
. By the previous case, we know that every natural isomorphism

F_{\underline{g}^{(n)}}(A)\xrightarrow{\sim}F_{\underline{g}^{(N)}}(A):=f^{*}A

obtained as the composite of inverse connection isomorphisms coincides with
\alpha_{\underline{g}^{(n)}}
. In particular, we have

\psi_{2}=\alpha_{\underline{g}^{(n)}}=\alpha_{\underline{g}^{(0)}}\circ\psi_{1}^{-1}.

The claim follows from this, since we have the equality

\phi=\psi_{2}\circ\psi_{1}=\alpha_{\underline{g}^{(n)}}\circ\psi_{1}=\alpha_{\underline{g}^{(0)}}.

•

Suppose only that at least one of the factors
\phi_{i}
is a direct connection isomorphism. We want to transform this factorization into one of the form considered in the previous case. To this end it suffices to show that, given two consecutive factors
\phi_{i}
and
\phi_{i+1}
with
\phi_{i}
inverse and
\phi_{i+1}
direct, it is possible to write the composite
\phi_{i+1}\circ\phi_{i}
in the form
\phi^{\prime}_{i+1}\circ\phi^{\prime}_{i}
with
\phi^{\prime}_{i}
direct and
\phi^{\prime}_{i+i}
inverse. We prove this by distinguishing the following cases:

–

If
\phi_{i}
and
\phi_{i+1}
are mutually inverse, they can be just cancelled out.

–

If
\phi_{i}
and
\phi_{i+1}
are not mutually inverse and the sets of morphisms where they act are disjoint from one another, then the corresponding natural transformations can be interchanged.

–

If
\phi_{i}
and
\phi_{i+1}
are not mutually inverse and the sets of morphisms where they act are not disjoint, then they fit into the bottom-right corner of a square of type (
5
). By axiom (
\mathscr{S}
-fib-1) we can replace them with the edges in the top-left corner of the same square.

Therefore the claim follows from the previous case.

This concludes the proof.
∎

Let us briefly review sections of
\mathscr{S}
-fibered categories: this notion will play a role in the discussion about unit constraints in
Section

5
and in the final part of
Section

8
.

Definition 1.4
.

Let
\mathbb{H}
be an
\mathscr{S}
-fibered category.

(1)

A
section

A
of
\mathbb{H}
over
\mathscr{S}
is the datum of

•

for every
S\in\mathscr{S}
, an object
A_{S}\in\mathbb{H}(S)
,

•

for every morphism
f:T\rightarrow S
in
\mathscr{S}
, an isomorphism in
\mathbb{H}(T)

A^{*}=A_{f}^{*}:f^{*}A_{S}\xrightarrow{\sim}A_{T}

satisfying the following condition:

(
\mathscr{S}
-sect)

For every pair of composable morphisms
f:T\rightarrow S
,
g:S\rightarrow V
in
\mathscr{S}
, the diagram in
\mathbb{H}(T)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}g^{*}A_{V}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}A_{S}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(gf)^{*}A_{V}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{g}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{conn}_{f,g}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{f}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{gf}^{*}}

is commutative.

(2)

Let
A
and
B
be two sections of
\mathbb{H}
. A
morphism of sections

w:A\rightarrow B
is the datum of

•

for every
S\in\mathscr{S}
, a morphism

w_{S}:A_{S}\rightarrow B_{S}

satisfying the following condition:

(mor-
\mathscr{S}
-sect)

For every morphism
f:T\rightarrow S
in
\mathscr{S}
, the diagram in
\mathbb{H}(T)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}A_{S}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}B_{S}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w_{S}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A^{*}_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B^{*}_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w_{T}}

is commutative.

Remark 1.5
.

There are natural notions of identity and of composition for morphisms between sections of a same
\mathscr{S}
-fibered categories
\mathbb{H}
. Of course, a morphism of sections
w:A\rightarrow B
is invertible with respect to this composition if and only if each morphism
w_{S}:A_{S}\rightarrow B_{S}
is an isomorphism in
\mathbb{H}(S)
for every
S\in\mathscr{S}
: this yields the notion of
isomorphism of sections
.

1.2.
Morphisms of fibered categories

In the second part of this section, we quickly review morphisms of
\mathscr{S}
-fibered categories. Let
\mathbb{H}_{1}
and
\mathbb{H}_{2}
be two
\mathscr{S}
-fibered categories as in
1.1
; for every pair of composable pair
(f,g)
in
\mathscr{S}
, we write the connection isomorphism at
(f,g)
for
\mathbb{H}_{1}
and
\mathbb{H}_{2}
as
\mathsf{conn}_{f,g}^{(1)}
and
\mathsf{conn}_{f,g}^{(2)}
, respectively.

Definition 1.6
.

(1)

A
morphism of
\mathscr{S}
-fibered categories

R:\mathbb{H}_{1}\rightarrow\mathbb{H}_{2}
is the datum of

•

for every
S\in\mathscr{S}
, a functor

R_{S}:\mathbb{H}_{1}(S)\rightarrow\mathbb{H}_{2}(S),

•

for every morphism
f:T\rightarrow S
in
\mathscr{S}
, a natural isomorphism of functors
\mathbb{H}_{1}(S)\rightarrow\mathbb{H}_{2}(T)

\theta=\theta_{f}:f^{*}R_{S}(A)\xrightarrow{\sim}R_{T}(f^{*}A),

called the
R
-transition isomorphism
along
f

such that the following condition is satisfied:

(mor-
\mathscr{S}
-fib)

For every pair of composable morphisms
f:T\rightarrow S
and
g:S\rightarrow V
in
\mathscr{S}
, the diagram of functors
\mathbb{H}_{1}(V)\rightarrow\mathbb{H}_{2}(T)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(gf)^{*}R_{V}(A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R_{T}((gf)^{*}A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}g^{*}R_{V}(A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}R_{S}(g^{*}A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R_{T}(f^{*}g^{*}A)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{gf}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{conn}^{(2)}_{f,g}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{conn}^{(1)}_{f,g}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{g}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{f}}

is commutative.

(2)

If we are already given a family of functors
\mathscr{R}:=\left\{R_{S}:\mathbb{H}_{1}(S)\rightarrow\mathbb{H}_{2}(S)\right\}_{S\in\mathscr{S}}
, we say that a collection of natural isomorphisms
\theta=\left\{\theta_{f}:f^{*}\circ R_{S}\xrightarrow{\sim}R_{T}\circ f^{*}\right\}_{f:T\rightarrow S}
satisfying condition (mor-
\mathscr{S}
-fib) defines an
\mathscr{S}
-structure
on the family
\mathscr{R}
.

Remark 1.7
.

Fix
S\in\mathscr{S}
. In view of axiom (
\mathscr{S}
-fib-0) from
1.1
, condition (mor-
\mathscr{S}
-fib) in the case where
f=g=\textup{id}_{S}
means that the natural isomorphism of functors
\mathbb{H}_{1}(S)\rightarrow\mathbb{H}_{2}(S)

\theta_{\textup{id}_{S}}:R_{S}(A)\xrightarrow{\sim}R_{S}(A)

is idempotent. Hence it must be the identity.

The full meaning of axiom (mor-
\mathscr{S}
-fib) is explained by the following result, which is the generalization of
1.3
to morphisms of
\mathscr{S}
-fibered categories:

Lemma 1.8
.

Let
R:\mathbb{H}_{1}\rightarrow\mathbb{H}_{2}
be a morphism of
\mathscr{S}
-fibered categories, and fix a morphism
f:T\rightarrow S
in
\mathscr{S}
. For every composable pair of composable tuples in
\mathscr{S}

\underline{g}=(g_{1},\dots,g_{r}),\quad\underline{h}=(h_{1},\dots,h_{s})

satisfying
h_{s}\circ\cdots\circ h_{1}\circ g_{r}\circ\cdots\circ g_{1}=f
, consider the functor
\mathbb{H}_{1}(S)\rightarrow\mathbb{H}_{2}(T)

F^{R}_{(\underline{g},\underline{h})}(A):=\underline{g}^{*}R_{T^{\prime}}(\underline{h}^{*}A)

where
T^{\prime}
denotes the domain of
h_{1}
. Then, given any two pairs of tuples
(\underline{g}^{(1)},\underline{h}^{(1)})
and
(\underline{g}^{(2)},\underline{h}^{(2)})
as above, all natural isomorphisms of functors
\mathbb{H}_{1}(S)\rightarrow\mathbb{H}_{2}(T)

F^{R}_{(\underline{g}^{(1)},\underline{h}^{(1)})}(A)\xrightarrow{\sim}F^{R}_{(\underline{g}^{(2)},\underline{h}^{(2)})}(A)

obtained by composing connection isomorphisms and
R
-transition isomorphisms (and inverses thereof) coincide.

It would be possible to prove this result via a detailed case-by-case analysis as done for
1.3
; however, the mixture of connection isomorphisms and
R
-transition isomorphisms would force us to consider more cases and introduce more reduction steps in the argument. We prefer to introduce an auxiliary construction which will reduce us directly to the situation of
1.3
, thereby avoiding all additional difficulties. To this end, we need to introduce some more notation:

Notation 1.9
.

•

We let
\mathscr{I}
be the category with two objects, denoted
1
and
2
, and exactly one non-identity arrow
r:2\rightarrow 1
.

•

Given a small category
\mathscr{S}
, we consider the product category
\mathscr{S}\times\mathscr{I}
. We write an object
\mathsf{S}\in\mathscr{S}\times\mathscr{I}
as a pair
(S;i)
with
S\in\mathscr{S}
and
i\in\left\{1,2\right\}
. For every
S\in\mathscr{S}
, we let
r_{S}
denote the morphism
(\textup{id}_{S},r):(S;2)\rightarrow(S;1)
.
Note that there exists a natural partition of morphisms in
\mathscr{S}\times\mathscr{I}
into the following three families, each of which is parameterized by the family of all morphisms
f:T\rightarrow S
in
\mathscr{S}
:

–

morphisms of the form
(f;1):(T;1)\rightarrow(S;1)
;

–

morphisms of the form
(f;2):(T;2)\rightarrow(S;2)
;

–

morphisms of the form
r_{S}\circ(f;2):(T;2)\rightarrow(S;1)
.

We will always use this presentation for morphisms in
\mathscr{S}\times\mathscr{I}
.

Construction 1.10
.

Let
R:\mathbb{H}_{1}\rightarrow\mathbb{H}_{2}
be a morphism of
\mathscr{S}
-fibered categories. Then, following
1.9
, we can define an
(\mathscr{S}\times\mathscr{I})
-fibered category
\mathbb{H}_{1}\coprod_{R}\mathbb{H}_{2}
out of it, as follows:

•

For every
(S;i)\in\mathscr{S}\times\mathscr{I}
, we set
(\mathbb{H}_{1}\coprod_{R}\mathbb{H}_{2})(S;i):=\mathbb{H}_{i}(S)
.

•

Given a morphism
\phi
in
\mathscr{S}\times\mathscr{I}
, we define the functor
\phi^{*}
according to the type of
\phi
:

–

if
\phi=(f;1):(T;1)\rightarrow(S;1)
, we set
\phi^{*}A:=f^{*}A
;

–

if
\phi=(f;2):(T;2)\rightarrow(S;2)
, we set
\phi^{*}B:=f^{*}B
;

–

if
\phi=r_{S}\circ(f;2)
, we set
\phi^{*}A:=f^{*}R_{S}(A)
.

•

Given two composable morphisms
\phi
and
\psi
in
\mathscr{S}\times\mathscr{I}
, we define the connection isomorphism
\mathsf{conn}_{\phi,\psi}
according to the type of
\phi
and
\psi
:

(i)

if
\phi=(f;1)
and
\psi=(g;1)
, we set

\mathsf{conn}_{\phi,\psi}(A):(gf)^{*}A\xrightarrow{\mathsf{conn}_{f,g}^{(1)}}f^{*}g^{*}A;

(ii)

if
\phi=(f;2)
and
\psi=(g;2)
, we set

\mathsf{conn}_{\phi,\psi}(B):(gf)^{*}B\xrightarrow{\mathsf{conn}_{f,g}^{(2)}}f^{*}g^{*}B;

(iii)

if
\phi=(f;2)
and
\psi=r_{V}\circ(g;2)
, we set

\mathsf{conn}_{\phi,\psi}(A):(gf)^{*}R_{V}(A)\xrightarrow{\mathsf{conn}_{f,g}^{(2)}}f^{*}g^{*}R_{V}(A);

(iv)

if
\phi=r_{S}\circ(f;2)
and
\psi=(g;1)
, we set

\mathsf{conn}_{\phi,\psi}:(gf)^{*}R_{S}(A)\xrightarrow{\mathsf{conn}_{f,g}^{(2)}}f^{*}g^{*}R_{V}(A)\xrightarrow{\theta_{g}}f^{*}R_{S}(g^{*}A).

Let us check that this really defines an
(\mathscr{S}\times\mathscr{I})
-fibered category.
It is clear that condition ((
\mathscr{S}\times\mathscr{I}
)-fib-0) is satisfied. We need to check that condition ((
\mathscr{S}\times\mathscr{I}
)-fib-1) holds as well: given three composable morphisms
\phi,\psi,\xi
in
\mathscr{S}\times\mathscr{I}
, we have to show that the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\xi\psi\phi)^{*}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi^{*}(\xi\psi)^{*}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\psi\phi)^{*}\xi^{*}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi^{*}\psi^{*}\xi^{*}A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{conn}_{\phi,\xi\psi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{conn}_{\phi,\xi\psi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{conn}_{\psi,\xi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{conn}_{\phi,\psi}}

is commutative. We have the following possibilities, parametrized by triples of composable morphisms
f:T\rightarrow S
,
g:S\rightarrow V
and
h:V\rightarrow W
in
\mathscr{S}
:

(1)

If
\phi=(f;1)
,
\psi=(g;1)
and
\xi=(h;1)
, we obtain the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(hgf)^{*}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}(hg)^{*}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(gf)^{*}h^{*}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}g^{*}h^{*}A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{conn}_{f,hg}^{(1)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{conn}_{gf,h}^{(1)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{conn}_{g,h}^{(1)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{conn}_{f,g}^{(1)}}

which is commutative by axiom (
\mathscr{S}
-fib-1).

(2)

If
\phi=(f;2)
,
\psi=(g;2)
and
\xi=(h;2)
, we obtain the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(hgf)^{*}B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}(hg)^{*}B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(gf)^{*}h^{*}B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}g^{*}h^{*}B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{conn}_{f,hg}^{(2)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{conn}_{gf,h}^{(2)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{conn}_{g,h}^{(2)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{conn}_{f,g}^{(2)}}

which is commutative by axiom (
\mathscr{S}
-fib-1).

(3)

If
\phi=(f;2)
,
\phi=(g;2)
and
\xi=r_{W}\circ(h;2)
, we obtain the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(hgf)^{*}R_{W}(A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}(hg)^{*}R_{W}(A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(gf)^{*}h^{*}R_{W}(A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}g^{*}h^{*}R_{W}(A)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{conn}_{f,hg}^{(2)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{conn}_{gf,h}^{(2)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{conn}_{g,h}^{(2)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{conn}_{f,g}^{(2)}}

which is again commutative by axiom (
\mathscr{S}
-fib-1).

(4)

If
\phi=(f;2)
,
\psi=r_{V}\circ(g;2)
and
\xi=(h;1)
, we obtain the outer part of the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(hgf)^{*}R_{W}(A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}(hg)^{*}R_{W}(A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(gf)^{*}h^{*}R_{W}(A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}g^{*}h^{*}R_{W}(A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(gf)^{*}R_{V}(h^{*}A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}g^{*}R_{V}(h^{*}A)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{conn}_{f,hg}^{(2)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{conn}_{gf,h}^{(2)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{conn}_{g,h}^{(2)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{conn}_{f,g}^{(2)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{h}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{h}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{conn}_{f,g}^{(2)}}

where the upper square is commutative by axiom (
\mathscr{S}
-fib-1) and the lower square is commutative by naturality.

(5)

Finally, if
\phi=r_{S}\circ(f;2)
,
\psi=(g;1)
and
\xi=(h;1)
, we obtain the outer part of the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(hgf)^{*}R_{W}(A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}(hg)^{*}R_{W}(A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}R_{S}((hg)^{*}A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(gf)^{*}h^{*}R_{W}(A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}g^{*}h^{*}R_{W}(A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(gf)^{*}R_{V}(h^{*}A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}g^{*}R_{V}(h^{*}A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}R_{S}(g^{*}h^{*}A)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{conn}_{f,hg}^{(2)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{conn}_{gf,h}^{(2)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{hg}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{conn}_{g,h}^{(2)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{conn}_{g,h}^{(1)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{conn}_{f,g}^{(2)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{h}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{h}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{conn}_{f,g}^{(2)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{g}}

where the upper-left piece is commutative by axiom (
\mathscr{S}
-fib-1), the lower-left piece is commutative by naturality, and the right-most piece is commutative by axiom (mor-
\mathscr{S}
-fib).

In fact, the argument shows that giving a morphism of
\mathscr{S}
-fibered categories
R:\mathbb{H}_{1}\rightarrow\mathbb{H}_{2}
is the same as giving an
(\mathscr{S}\times\mathscr{I})
-fibered category
\mathbb{H}
satisfying
\mathbb{H}|_{(\mathscr{S};i)}=\mathbb{H}_{i}
for
i=1,2
and
r_{S}^{*}=R_{S}
for all
S\in\mathscr{S}
.

Proof of
1.8
.

As a consequence of
1.10
, this is a particular case of
1.3
over the base category
\mathscr{S}\times\mathscr{I}
.
∎

We conclude this preliminary discussion by reviewing the notion of image of a section along a morphism of
\mathscr{S}
-fibered categories.

Construction 1.11
.

Let
R:\mathbb{H}_{1}\rightarrow\mathbb{H}_{2}
be a morphism of
\mathscr{S}
-fibered categories, and let
A
be a section of
\mathbb{H}_{1}
over
\mathscr{S}
.
We define the
image
of
A
under
R
to be the section
R(A)
of
\mathbb{H}_{2}
over
\mathscr{S}
defined as follows:

•

for every
S\in\mathscr{S}
, the object
R(A)_{S}\in\mathbb{H}_{2}(S)
is just
R_{S}(A_{S})
;

•

for every morphism
f:T\rightarrow S
in
\mathscr{S}
, the isomorphism in
\mathbb{H}_{2}(T)

R(A)_{f}^{*}:f^{*}R(A)_{S}\xrightarrow{\sim}R(A)_{T}

is the composite

f^{*}R_{S}(A_{S})\xrightarrow{\theta_{f}}R_{T}(f^{*}A_{S})\xrightarrow{\sim}R_{T}(A_{T}).

Let us check that this definition indeed satisfies condition (
\mathscr{S}
-sect): given two composable morphisms
f:T\rightarrow S
and
g:S\rightarrow V
in
\mathscr{S}
, we have to show that the diagram in
\mathbb{H}_{2}(T)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(gf)^{*}R(A)_{V}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R(A)_{T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}g^{*}R(A)_{V}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}R(A)_{S}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R(A)_{T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R(A)_{gf}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{conn}_{f,g}^{(2)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{conn}_{f,g}^{(2)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R(A)_{g}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R(A)_{f}^{*}}

is commutative. Expanding the definitions, we obtain the outer part of the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(gf)^{*}R_{V}(A_{V})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R_{T}((gf)^{*}A_{V})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R_{T}(A_{T})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R_{T}(f^{*}g^{*}A_{V})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}g^{*}R_{V}(A_{V})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}R_{S}(g^{*}A_{V})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}R_{S}(A_{S})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R_{T}(f^{*}A_{S})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{gf}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{conn}_{f,g}^{(2)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{gf}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{conn}_{f,g}^{(1)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{g}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{g}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{g}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{f}^{*}}

where the lower central piece is commutative by naturality, the left-most piece is commutative by axiom (mor-
\mathscr{S}
-fib), and the right-most piece is commutative by axiom (
\mathscr{S}
-sect). This proves the claim.

2.
Internal and external tensor structures

From now henceforth, we assume that the small category
\mathscr{S}
admits binary products; in most concrete applications
\mathscr{S}
also happens to possess a terminal object, but this is not necessary for our constructions.

From now until the end of
Section

7
we also fix an
\mathscr{S}
-fibered category
\mathbb{H}
.
In the present section, we start our investigations about monoidal
\mathscr{S}
-fibered categories by introducing the notions of internal and external tensor structure on
\mathbb{H}
together with the corresponding notions of equivalence.

Notation 2.1
.

From now on, we simplify the notation by systematically adopting the following conventions:

•

We write all connection isomorphisms of
\mathscr{S}
-fibered categories as equalities (this is legitimate in view of
1.3
).

•

We write all transition isomorphisms
\theta_{f}
associated to a morphism of
\mathscr{S}
-fibered categories simply as
\theta
.
More generally, when a structure on
\mathscr{S}
-fibered category is defined by a family of natural isomorphisms parameterized by objects or morphisms of
\mathscr{S}
, we often indicate all members of this family by the same symbol, without mentioning the specific parameter involved. In particular, this will be applied to all the notation concerning tensor structures.

2.1.
Tensor structures and their equivalences

To begin with, we introduce internal and external tensor structures and equivalences thereof. In order to keep the exposition as neat as possible, it is convenient to start from the coarsest possible kind of structure including only monoidality isomorphisms: in particular, we do not include associativity constraints in the picture from the beginning but rather regard them as one possible additional structure (on the same level as commutativity and unit constraints) and treat them separately in
Section

3
.

Definition 2.2
.

(1)

An
internal tensor structure

(\otimes,m)
on
\mathbb{H}
is the datum of

•

for every
S\in\mathscr{S}
, a functor

-\otimes-=-\otimes_{S}-:\mathbb{H}(S)\times\mathbb{H}(S)\rightarrow\mathbb{H}(S),

called the
internal tensor product functor
over
S
,

•

for every morphism
f:T\rightarrow S
in
\mathscr{S}
, a natural isomorphism of functors
\mathbb{H}(S)\times\mathbb{H}(S)\rightarrow\mathbb{H}(T)

m=m_{f}:f^{*}A\otimes f^{*}B\xrightarrow{\sim}f^{*}(A\otimes B),

called the
internal monoidality isomorphism
along
f

satisfying the following condition:

(
m
ITS)

For every pair of composable morphisms
f:T\rightarrow S
and
g:S\rightarrow V
in
\mathscr{S}
, the diagram of functors
\mathbb{H}(V)\times\mathbb{H}(V)\rightarrow\mathbb{H}(T)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(gf)^{*}A\otimes(gf)^{*}B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(gf)^{*}(A\otimes B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}g^{*}A\otimes f^{*}g^{*}B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}(g^{*}A\otimes g^{*}B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}g^{*}(A\otimes B)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}

is commutative.

(2)

Let
(\otimes,m)
and
(\otimes^{\prime},m^{\prime})
be two internal tensor structures on
\mathbb{H}
. An
equivalence of internal tensor structures

e:(\otimes,m)\xrightarrow{\sim}(\otimes^{\prime},m^{\prime})
is the datum of

•

for every
S\in\mathscr{S}
, a natural isomorphism of functors
\mathbb{H}(S)\times\mathbb{H}(S)\rightarrow\mathbb{H}(S)

e=e_{S}:A\otimes B\xrightarrow{\sim}A\otimes^{\prime}B

satisfying the following condition:

(eq-ITS)

For every morphism
f:T\rightarrow S
in
\mathscr{S}
, the diagram of functors
\mathbb{H}(S)\times\mathbb{H}(S)\rightarrow\mathbb{H}(T)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}A\otimes f^{*}B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}(A\otimes B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}A\otimes^{\prime}f^{*}B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}(A\otimes^{\prime}B)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m^{\prime}}

is commutative.

Remark 2.3
.

Fix
S\in\mathscr{S}
. In view of axiom (
\mathscr{S}
-fib-0) of
1.1
, condition (
m
ITS) in the case where
f=g=\textup{id}_{S}
means that the natural isomorphism of functors
\mathbb{H}(S)\times\mathbb{H}(S)\rightarrow\mathbb{H}(S)

m_{\textup{id}_{S}}:A\otimes B\xrightarrow{\sim}A\otimes B

is idempotent. Hence it must be the identity.

The following result clarifies the meaning of axiom (
m
ITS): it guarantees that, between any two given iterations of tensor product functors and inverse image functors, it is possible to construct at most one natural isomorphism by combining connection isomorphisms and internal monoidality isomorphisms in any possible order. As the reader might expect, this is just another variant of
1.3
.

Lemma 2.4
.

Let
(\otimes,m)
be an internal tensor structure on
\mathbb{H}
, and let
f:T\rightarrow S
be a morphism in
\mathscr{S}
. For every composable pair of composable tuples in
\mathscr{S}

\underline{g}=(g_{1},\dots,g_{r}),\quad\underline{h}=(h_{1},\dots,h_{s})

satisfying
h_{s}\circ\dots\circ h_{1}\circ g_{r}\circ\dots\circ g_{1}=f
, consider the functor
\mathbb{H}(S)\times\mathbb{H}(S)\rightarrow\mathbb{H}(T)

F^{\otimes}_{(\underline{g},\underline{h})}(A,B):=\underline{g}^{*}(\underline{h}^{*}A\otimes\underline{h}^{*}B).

Then, given any two pairs of tuples
(\underline{g}^{(1)},\underline{h}^{(1)})
and
(\underline{g}^{(2)},\underline{h}^{(2)})
as above, all possible isomorphisms of functors
\mathbb{H}(S)\times\mathbb{H}(S)\rightarrow\mathbb{H}(T)

F^{\otimes}_{(\underline{g}^{(1)},\underline{h}^{(1)})}(A,B)\xrightarrow{\sim}F^{\otimes}_{(\underline{g}^{(2)},\underline{h}^{(2)})}(A,B)

obtained by composing connection isomorphisms and internal monoidality isomorphisms (and inverses thereof) coincide.

It would be possible to prove this result directly; however, the argument would be quite cumbersome due to the mixture of connection isomorphisms and internal monoidality isomorphisms. We prefer to reduce ourselves directly to the setting of usual fibered categories considered in
Section

1
via the following auxiliary construction:

Construction 2.5
.

Let
(\otimes,m)
be an internal tensor structure on
\mathbb{H}
. Then, following
1.9
, we can construct a
(\mathscr{S}\times\mathscr{I})
-fibered category
\mathbb{H}^{\otimes}
out of it, as follows:

•

For every
S\in\mathscr{S}
we set
\mathbb{H}^{\otimes}(S;1):=\mathbb{H}(S)
and
\mathbb{H}^{\otimes}(S;2):=\mathbb{H}(S)\times\mathbb{H}(S)
.

•

Given a morphism
\phi
in
\mathscr{S}\times\mathscr{I}
, we define the functor
\phi^{*}
according to the type of
\phi
:

–

if
\phi=(f;1):(T;1)\rightarrow(S;1)
, we set
\phi^{*}A:=f^{*}A
;

–

if
\phi=(f;2):(T;2)\rightarrow(S;2)
, we set
\phi^{*}(A,B):=(f^{*}A,f^{*}B)
;

–

if
\phi=r_{S}\circ(f;2)
, we set
\phi^{*}(A,B):=f^{*}(A\otimes B)
.

•

Give two composable morphisms
\phi
and
\psi
in
\mathscr{S}\times\mathscr{I}
, we define the connection isomorphism
\mathsf{conn}_{\phi,\psi}
according to the type of
\phi
and
\psi
:

(i)

if
\phi=(f;1)
and
\psi=(g;1)
, we set

\mathsf{conn}_{\phi,\psi}(A):(gf)^{*}A\xrightarrow{\mathsf{conn}_{f,g}}f^{*}g^{*}A;

(ii)

if
\phi=(f;2)
and
\psi=(g;2)
, we set

\mathsf{conn}_{\phi,\psi}(A,B):((gf)^{*}A,(gf)^{*}B)\xrightarrow{(\mathsf{conn}_{f,g},\mathsf{conn}_{f,g})}(f^{*}g^{*}A,f^{*}g^{*}B);

(iii)

if
\phi=(f;2)
and
\psi=r_{V}\circ(g;2)
, we set

\mathsf{conn}_{\phi,\psi}(A,B):(gf)^{*}(A\otimes B)\xrightarrow{\mathsf{conn}_{f,g}}f^{*}g^{*}(A\otimes B);

(iv)

if
\phi=r_{S}\circ(f;2)
and
\psi=(g;1)
, we set

\mathsf{conn}_{\phi,\psi}(A,B):(gf)^{*}(A\otimes B)\xrightarrow{\mathsf{conn}_{f,g}}f^{*}g^{*}(A\otimes B)\xleftarrow{m_{g}}f^{*}(g^{*}A\otimes g^{*}B).

Let us check that this construction indeed defines a
(\mathscr{S}\times\mathscr{I})
-fibered category. The validity of condition ((
\mathscr{S}\times\mathscr{I}
)-fib-0) follows trivially from axiom (
\mathscr{S}
-fib-0). Let us check that condition ((
\mathscr{S}\times\mathscr{I}
)-fib-1) holds as well: given three composable morphisms
\phi,\psi,\xi
in
\mathscr{S}\times\mathscr{I}
, we have to show that the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\xi\psi\phi)^{*}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi^{*}(\xi\psi)^{*}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\psi\phi)^{*}\xi^{*}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi^{*}\psi^{*}\xi^{*}A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{conn}_{\phi,\xi\psi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{conn}_{\psi\phi,\xi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{conn}_{\psi,\xi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{conn}_{\phi,\psi}}

is commutative. We have the following possibilities, parametrized by triples of composable morphisms
f:T\rightarrow S
,
g:S\rightarrow V
and
h:V\rightarrow W
in
\mathscr{S}
:

(1)

If
\phi=(f;1)
,
\psi=(g;1)
and
\xi=(h;1)
, we obtain the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(hgf)^{*}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}(hg)^{*}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(gf)^{*}h^{*}A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}g^{*}h^{*}A}

which is commutative by axiom (
\mathscr{S}
-fib-1).

(2)

If
\phi=(f;2)
,
\psi=(g;2)
and
\xi=(h;2)
, we obtain the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces((hgf)^{*}A,(hgf)^{*}B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(f^{*}(hg)^{*}A,f^{*}(hg)^{*}B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces((gf)^{*}h^{*}A,(gf)^{*}h^{*}B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(f^{*}g^{*}h^{*}A,f^{*}g^{*}h^{*}B)}

which is commutative by axiom (
\mathscr{S}
-fib-1).

(3)

If
\phi=(f;2)
,
\phi=(g;2)
and
\xi=r_{W}\circ(h;2)
, we obtain the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(hgf)^{*}(A\otimes B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}(hg)^{*}(A\otimes B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(gf)^{*}h^{*}(A\otimes B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}g^{*}h^{*}(A\otimes B)}

which is again commutative by axiom (
\mathscr{S}
-fib-1).

(4)

If
\phi=(f;2)
,
\psi=r_{V}\circ(g;2)
and
\xi=(h;1)
, we obtain (the outer rectangle of) the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(hgf)^{*}(A\otimes B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}(hg)^{*}(A\otimes B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(gf)^{*}h^{*}(A\otimes B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}g^{*}h^{*}(A\otimes B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(gf)^{*}(h^{*}A\otimes h^{*}B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}g^{*}(h^{*}A\otimes h^{*}B)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}

where the upper square is commutative by axiom (
\mathscr{S}
-fib-1) and the lower square is commutative by naturality.

(5)

If
\phi=r_{S}\circ(f;2)
,
\psi=(g;1)
and
\xi=(h;1)
, we obtain (the outer rectangle of) the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(hgf)^{*}(A\otimes B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}(hg)^{*}(A\otimes B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}((hg)^{*}A\otimes(hg)^{*}B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(gf)^{*}h^{*}(A\otimes B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}g^{*}h^{*}(A\otimes B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(gf)^{*}(h^{*}A\otimes h^{*}B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}g^{*}(h^{*}A\otimes h^{*}B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}(g^{*}h^{*}A\otimes g^{*}h^{*}B)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}

where the upper-left piece is commutative by axiom (
\mathscr{S}
-fib-1), the lower-left piece is commutative by naturality, and the right-most piece is commutative by axiom (
m
ITS).

Proof of
2.4
.

As a consequence of
2.5
, this is just a particular case of
1.3
.
∎

In a similar way, we now introduce external tensor structures and equivalences thereof.

Definition 2.6
.

(1)

An
external tensor structure

(\boxtimes,m)
on
\mathbb{H}
is the datum of

•

for every
S_{1},S_{2}\in\mathscr{S}
, a functor

-\boxtimes-=-\boxtimes_{S_{1},S_{2}}-:\mathbb{H}(S_{1})\times\mathbb{H}(S_{2})\rightarrow\mathbb{H}(S_{1}\times S_{2}),

called the
external tensor product functor
over
(S_{1},S_{2})
,

•

for every choice of morphisms
f_{i}:T_{i}\rightarrow S_{i}
in
\mathscr{S}
,
i=1,2
, a natural isomorphism of functors
\mathbb{H}(S_{1})\times\mathbb{H}(S_{2})\rightarrow\mathbb{H}(T_{1}\times T_{2})

m=m_{f_{1},f_{2}}:f_{1}^{*}A_{1}\boxtimes f_{2}^{*}A_{2}\xrightarrow{\sim}(f_{1}\times f_{2})^{*}(A_{1}\boxtimes A_{2}),

called the
external monoidality isomorphism
along
(f_{1},f_{2})

satisfying the following condition:

(
m
ETS)

For every choice of composable morphisms
f_{i}:T_{i}\rightarrow S_{i}
,
g_{i}:S_{i}\rightarrow V_{i}
,
i=1,2
, the diagram of functors
\mathbb{H}(V_{1})\times\mathbb{H}(V_{2})\rightarrow\mathbb{H}(T_{1}\times T_{2})

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(g_{1}f_{1})^{*}A_{1}\boxtimes(g_{2}f_{2})^{*}A_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(g_{1}f_{1}\times g_{2}f_{2})^{*}(A_{1}\boxtimes A_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}^{*}g_{1}^{*}A_{1}\boxtimes f_{2}^{*}g_{2}^{*}A_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(f_{1}\times f_{2})^{*}(g_{1}^{*}A_{1}\boxtimes g_{2}^{*}A_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(f_{1}\times f_{2})^{*}(g_{1}\times g_{2})^{*}(A_{1}\boxtimes A_{2})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}

is commutative.

(2)

Let
(\boxtimes^{\prime},m^{\prime})
and
(\boxtimes,m)
be two external tensor structures on
\mathbb{H}
. An
equivalence of external tensor structures

e:(\boxtimes^{\prime},m^{\prime})\xrightarrow{\sim}(\boxtimes,m)
is the datum of

•

for every
S_{1},S_{2}\in\mathscr{S}
, a natural isomorphism of functors
\mathbb{H}(S_{1})\times\mathbb{H}(S_{2})\rightarrow\mathbb{H}(S_{1}\times S_{2})

e=e_{S_{1},S_{2}}:A_{1}\boxtimes^{\prime}A_{2}\xrightarrow{\sim}A_{1}\boxtimes A_{2}

satisfying the following condition:

(eq-ETS)

For every pair of morphisms
f_{i}:T_{i}\rightarrow S_{i}
in
\mathscr{S}
, the diagram of functors
\mathbb{H}(S_{1})\times\mathbb{H}(S_{2})\rightarrow\mathbb{H}(T_{1}\times T_{2})

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}^{*}A_{1}\boxtimes^{\prime}f_{2}^{*}A_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(f_{1}\times f_{2})^{*}(A_{1}\boxtimes^{\prime}A_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}^{*}A_{1}\boxtimes f_{2}^{*}A_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(f_{1}\times f_{2})^{*}(A_{1}\boxtimes A_{2})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}

is commutative.

Remark 2.7
.

For
i=1,2
fix
S_{i}\in\mathscr{S}
. In view of axiom (
\mathscr{S}
-fib-0) of
1.1
, condition (
m
ETS) in the case where
f_{i}=g_{i}=\textup{id}_{S_{i}}
means that the natural isomorphism of functors
\mathbb{H}(S_{1})\times\mathbb{H}(S_{2})\rightarrow\mathbb{H}(S_{1}\times S_{2})

m_{\textup{id}_{S_{1}},\textup{id}_{S_{2}}}:A_{1}\boxtimes A_{2}\xrightarrow{\sim}A_{1}\boxtimes A_{2}

is idempotent. Hence it must be the identity.

As we now explain, the result of
2.4
has an analogue in the setting of external tensor structures, which clarifies the meaning of axiom (
m
ETS): between any two given iterations of external tensor product functors and inverse image functors (respecting the single factors), it is possible to construct at most one natural isomorphism by combining connection isomorphisms and external monoidality isomorphisms in any possible order.

Lemma 2.8
.

Let
(\boxtimes,m)
be an external tensor structure on
\mathbb{H}
and, for
i=1,2
, let
f_{i}:T_{i}\rightarrow S_{i}
be a morphism in
\mathscr{S}
. For every choice of two composable pairs of composable tuples in
\mathscr{S}

\underline{g}_{i}=(g_{i,1},\dots,g_{i,r}),\quad\underline{h}_{i}=(h_{i,1},\dots,h_{i,s})\qquad(i=1,2)

satisfying
h_{i,s}\circ\dots\circ h_{i,1}\circ g_{i,r}\circ\dots\circ g_{i,1}=f_{i}
, consider the functor
\mathbb{H}(S_{1})\times\mathbb{H}(S_{2})\rightarrow\mathbb{H}(T_{1}\times T_{2})

F^{\boxtimes}_{(\underline{g}_{1},\underline{h}_{1}),(\underline{g}_{2},\underline{h}_{2})}(A_{1},A_{2}):=(\underline{g}_{1}\times\underline{g}_{2})^{*}(\underline{h}_{1}^{*}A_{1}\boxtimes\underline{h}_{2}^{*}A_{2}).

Then, given, for
i=1,2
, any two pairs of tuples
(\underline{g}_{i}^{(1)},\underline{h}_{i}^{(1)})
and
(\underline{g}_{i}^{(2)},\underline{h}_{i}^{(2)})
as above, all isomorphisms of functors
\mathbb{H}(S_{1})\times\mathbb{H}(S_{2})\rightarrow\mathbb{H}(T_{1}\times T_{2})

F^{\boxtimes}_{(\underline{g}_{1}^{(1)},\underline{h}_{1}^{(1)}),(\underline{g}_{2}^{(1)},\underline{h}_{2}^{(1)})}\xrightarrow{\sim}F^{\boxtimes}_{(\underline{g}_{1}^{(2)},\underline{h}_{1}^{(2)}),(\underline{g}_{2}^{(2)},\underline{h}_{2}^{(2)})}

obtained by composing connection isomorphisms and external monoidality isomorphisms (and inverses thereof) coincide.

As in the case of internal tensor structures, we can reduce the proof of this result to the setting of usual fibered categories considered in
Section

1
via an auxiliary construction:

Construction 2.9
.

Let
(\boxtimes,m)
be an internal tensor structure on
\mathbb{H}
. Then, following
1.9
, we can construct an
(\mathscr{S}^{2}\times\mathscr{I})
-fibered category
\mathbb{H}^{\boxtimes}
out of it, as follows:

•

For every
S_{1},S_{2}\in\mathscr{S}
we set
\mathbb{H}^{\boxtimes}(S_{1},S_{2};1):=\mathbb{H}(S_{1})\times\mathbb{H}(S_{2})
and
\mathbb{H}^{\boxtimes}(S_{1},S_{2};2):=\mathbb{H}(S_{1}\times S_{2})
.

•

Given a morphism
\phi
in
\mathscr{S}^{2}\times\mathscr{I}
, we define the functor
\phi^{*}
according to the type of
\phi
:

–

if
\phi=(f_{1},f_{2};1):(T_{1},T_{2};1)\rightarrow(S_{1},S_{2};1)
, we set
\phi^{*}(A_{1},A_{2}):=(f_{1}^{*}A_{1},f_{2}^{*}A_{2})
;

–

if
\phi=(f,2):(T_{1},T_{2};2)\rightarrow(S_{1},S_{2};2)
, we set
\phi^{*}A:=(f_{1}\times f_{2})^{*}A
;

–

if
\phi=r_{S_{1},S_{2}}\circ(f_{1},f_{2};2)
, we set
\phi^{*}(A_{1},A_{2}):=(f_{1}\times f_{2})^{*}(A_{1}\boxtimes A_{2})
.

•

Given two composable morphisms
\phi
and
\psi
in
\mathscr{S}^{2}\times\mathscr{I}
, we define the connection isomorphism
\mathsf{conn}_{\phi,\psi}
according to the type of
\phi
and
\psi
:

(i)

if
\phi=(f_{1},f_{2};1)
and
\psi=(g_{1},g_{2};1)
, we set

\mathsf{conn}_{\phi,\psi}(A_{1},A_{2}):((g_{1}f_{1})^{*}A_{1},(g_{2}f_{2})^{*}A_{2})\xrightarrow{(\mathsf{conn}_{f_{1},g_{1}},\mathsf{conn}_{f_{2},g_{2}})}(f_{1}^{*}g_{1}^{*}A_{1},f_{2}^{*}g_{2}^{*}A_{2});

(ii)

if
\phi=(f_{1},f_{2};2)
and
\psi=(g_{1},g_{2};2)
, we set

\mathsf{conn}_{\phi,\psi}(A):(g_{1}f_{1}\times g_{2}f_{2})^{*}A\xrightarrow{\mathsf{conn}_{f_{1}\times f_{2},g_{1}\times g_{2}}}(f_{1}\times f_{2})^{*}(g_{1}\times g_{2})^{*}A;

(iii)

if
\phi=(f,2)
and
\psi=r_{V}\circ(g,2)
, we set

\mathsf{conn}_{\phi,\psi}(A_{1},A_{2}):(g_{1}f_{1}\times g_{2}f_{2})^{*}(A_{1}\boxtimes A_{2})\xrightarrow{\mathsf{conn}_{f_{1}\times f_{2},g_{1}\times g_{2}}}(f_{1}\times f_{2})^{*}(g_{1}\times g_{2})^{*}(A_{1}\boxtimes A_{2});

(iv)

if
\phi=r_{S}\circ(f,2)
and
\psi=(g,1)
, we set

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{conn}_{\phi,\psi}(A_{1},A_{2}):}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(g_{1}f_{1}\times g_{2}f_{2})^{*}(A_{1}\boxtimes A_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(f_{1}\times f_{2})^{*}(g_{1}\times g_{2})^{*}(A_{1}\boxtimes A_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(f_{1}\times f_{2})^{*}(g_{1}^{*}A_{1}\boxtimes g_{2}^{*}A_{2}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{conn}_{f_{1}\times f_{2},g_{1}\times g_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{g_{1},g_{2}}}

Arguing in the same way as in
2.5
, one can check that this construction indeed defines a
(\mathscr{S}^{2}\times\mathscr{I})
-fibered category; we omit the details.

Proof of
2.8
.

As a consequence of
2.9
, this is just a particular case of
1.3
.
∎

2.2.
Correspondence between internal and external tensor structures

We now move towards the main goal of the present section: we want to prove that, modulo equivalence, giving an internal tensor structure on
\mathbb{H}
is the same as giving an external tensor structure on it.

The following result shows how to extend the classical formula (
2
) to a construction from internal to external tensor structures:

Lemma 2.10
.

Let
(\otimes,m)
be an internal tensor structure on
\mathbb{H}
. Then, assigning

•

to every
S_{1},S_{2}\in\mathscr{S}
, the functor

-\boxtimes-=-\boxtimes_{S_{1},S_{2}}-:\mathbb{H}(S_{1})\times\mathbb{H}(S_{2})\rightarrow\mathbb{H}(S_{1}\times S_{2})

defined by the formula

A_{1}\boxtimes A_{1}:=pr^{12*}_{1}A_{1}\otimes pr^{12*}_{2}A_{2},

•

to every choice of morphisms
f_{i}:T_{i}\rightarrow S_{i}
in
\mathscr{S}
,
i=1,2
, the natural isomorphism of functors
\mathbb{H}(S_{1})\times\mathbb{H}(S_{2})\rightarrow\mathbb{H}(T_{1}\times T_{2})

m^{e}=m^{e}_{f_{1},f_{2}}:f_{1}^{*}A_{1}\boxtimes f_{2}^{*}A_{2}\xrightarrow{\sim}(f_{1}\times f_{2})^{*}(A_{1}\boxtimes A_{2})

defined as the composite

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}^{*}A_{1}\boxtimes f_{2}^{*}A_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(f_{1}\times f_{2})^{*}(A_{1}\boxtimes A_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces pr^{12,*}_{1}f_{1}^{*}A_{1}\otimes pr^{12,*}_{2}f_{2}^{*}A_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(f_{1}\times f_{2})^{*}pr^{12,*}_{1}A_{1}\otimes(f_{1}\times f_{2})^{*}pr^{12,*}_{2}A_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(f_{1}\times f_{2})^{*}(pr^{12,*}_{1}A_{1}\otimes pr^{12,*}_{2}A_{2})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}

defines an external tensor structure
(\boxtimes,m^{e})
on
\mathbb{H}
.

Proof.

Let us check that this definition satisfies condition (
m
ETS): given, for
i=1,2
, two composable morphisms
f_{i}:T_{i}\rightarrow S_{i}
and
g_{i}:S_{i}\rightarrow V_{i}
in
\mathscr{S}
, we have to show that the diagram of functors
\mathbb{H}(V_{1})\times\mathbb{H}(V_{2})\rightarrow\mathbb{H}(T_{1}\times T_{2})

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(g_{1}f_{1})^{*}A_{1}\boxtimes(g_{2}f_{2})^{*}A_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(g_{1}f_{1}\times g_{2}f_{2})^{*}(A_{1}\boxtimes A_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}^{*}g_{1}^{*}A_{1}\boxtimes f_{1}^{*}g_{1}^{*}A_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(f_{1}\times f_{2})^{*}(g_{1}^{*}A_{1}\boxtimes g_{2}^{*}A_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(f_{1}\times f_{2})^{*}(g_{1}\times g_{2})^{*}(A_{1}\boxtimes A_{2})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m^{e}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m^{e}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m^{e}}

is commutative. Unwinding the definitions in terms of the internal tensor product, we obtain the more explicit diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(g_{12}f_{12})^{*}pr^{12,*}_{1}A_{1}\otimes(g_{12}f_{12})^{*}pr^{12,*}_{2}A_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(g_{1}f_{1}\times g_{2}f_{2})^{*}(pr^{12,*}_{1}A_{1}\otimes pr^{12,*}_{2}A_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces pr^{12,*}_{1}(g_{1}f_{1})^{*}A_{1}\otimes pr^{12,*}_{2}(g_{2}f_{2})^{*}A_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{12}^{*}g_{12}^{*}(pr^{12,*}_{1}A_{1}\otimes pr^{12,*}_{2}A_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces pr^{12,*}_{1}f_{1}^{*}g_{1}^{*}A_{1}\otimes pr^{12,*}_{2}f_{2}^{*}g_{2}^{*}A_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{12}^{*}(g_{12}^{*}pr^{12,*}_{1}A_{1}\otimes g_{12}^{*}pr^{12,*}_{2}A_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{12}^{*}pr^{12,*}_{1}g_{1}^{*}A_{1}\otimes f_{12}^{*}pr^{12,*}_{2}g_{2}^{*}A_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{12}^{*}(pr^{12,*}_{1}g_{1}^{*}A_{1}\otimes pr^{12,*}_{2}g_{2}^{*}A_{2})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}

that we decompose as

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{12}^{*}g_{12}^{*}pr^{12*}_{1}A_{1}\otimes f_{12}^{*}g_{12}^{*}pr^{12*}_{2}A_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}

Here, the left-most piece is commutative by
1.3
, the upper-right piece is commutative by axiom (
m
ITS), and the lower-right piece is commutative by naturality. This concludes the proof.
∎

Conversely, the following result shows how to turn the classical formula (
3
) into a construction from external to internal tensor structures:

Lemma 2.11
.

Let
(\boxtimes,m)
be an external tensor structure on
\mathbb{H}
. Then, assigning

•

to every
S\in\mathscr{S}
, the functor

-\otimes-=-\otimes_{S}-:\mathbb{H}(S)\times\mathbb{H}(S)\rightarrow\mathbb{H}(S)

defined by the formula

A\otimes B:=\Delta_{S}^{*}(A\boxtimes B),

•

to every morphism
f:T\rightarrow S
in
\mathscr{S}
, the natural isomorphism of functors
\mathbb{H}(S)\times\mathbb{H}(S)\rightarrow\mathbb{H}(T)

m^{i}=m^{i}_{f}:f^{*}A\otimes f^{*}B\xrightarrow{\sim}f^{*}(A\otimes B)

defined as the composite

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}A\otimes f^{*}B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}(A\otimes B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{T}^{*}(f^{*}A\boxtimes f^{*}B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{T}^{*}(f\times f)^{*}(A\boxtimes B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}\Delta_{S}^{*}(A\boxtimes B)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}

defines an internal tensor structure
(\otimes,m^{i})
on
\mathbb{H}
.

Proof.

Let us check that this definition satisfies condition (
m
ITS): given two composable morphisms
f:T\rightarrow S
and
g:S\rightarrow V
, we have to show that the diagram of functors
\mathbb{H}(V)\times\mathbb{H}(V)\rightarrow\mathbb{H}(T)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(gf)^{*}A\otimes(gf)^{*}B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(gf)^{*}(A\otimes B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}g^{*}A\otimes f^{*}g^{*}B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}(g^{*}A\otimes g^{*}B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}g^{*}(A\otimes B)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m^{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m^{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m^{i}}

is commutative. Unwinding the definitions in terms of the external tensor product, we obtain the more explicit diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{T}^{*}((gf)^{*}A\otimes(gf)^{*}B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{T}^{*}(gf\times gf)^{*}(A\boxtimes B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(gf)^{*}\Delta_{V}^{*}(A\boxtimes B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{T}^{*}(f^{*}g^{*}A\boxtimes f^{*}g^{*}B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}g^{*}\Delta_{V}^{*}(A\boxtimes B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{T}^{*}(f\times f)^{*}(g^{*}A\boxtimes g^{*}B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}\Delta_{S}^{*}(g^{*}A\boxtimes g^{*}B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}\Delta_{S}^{*}(g\times g)^{*}(A\boxtimes B)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}

that we decompose as

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{T}^{*}(f\times f)^{*}(g\times g)^{*}(A\boxtimes B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}

Here, the upper-left piece is commutative by axiom (
m
ETS), the upper-right pice is commutative by
1.3
, and the lower piece is commutative by naturality. This concludes the proof.
∎

We can finally prove the main result of this section, asserting that the two constructions just described are canonically mutually inverse modulo equivalence:

Proposition 2.12
.

The constructions of
2.10
and
2.11
canonically define mutually inverse bijections between equivalence classes of internal and external tensor structures on
\mathbb{H}
.

Proof.

Start from an internal tensor structure
(\otimes,m)
on
\mathbb{H}
, and let
(\otimes^{\prime},m^{\prime})
denote the internal tensor structure obtained from it by applying first
2.10
and then
2.11
: concretely, for every
S\in\mathscr{S}
, it is defined by the formula

A\otimes^{\prime}B:=\Delta_{S}^{*}(pr^{12,*}_{1}A\otimes pr^{12,*}_{2}B).

We want to construct a canonical equivalence
e:(\otimes,m)\xrightarrow{\sim}(\otimes^{\prime},m^{\prime})
.
To this end, for every
S\in\mathscr{S}
we define an isomorphism of functors
\mathbb{H}(S)\times\mathbb{H}(S)\rightarrow\mathbb{H}(S)

e=e_{S}:A\otimes B\xrightarrow{\sim}A\otimes^{\prime}B

by taking the composite

A\otimes B=\Delta_{S}^{*}pr^{12,*}_{1}A\otimes\Delta_{S}^{*}pr^{12,*}_{2}B\xrightarrow{m}\Delta_{S}^{*}(pr^{12,*}_{1}A\otimes pr^{12,*}_{2}B)=:A\otimes^{\prime}B.

We claim that, as
S
varies in
\mathscr{S}
, these isomorphisms satisfy condition (eq-ITS): given a morphism
f:T\rightarrow S
in
\mathscr{S}
, we have to check that the diagram of functors
\mathbb{H}(S)\times\mathbb{H}(S)\rightarrow\mathbb{H}(T)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}A\otimes f^{*}B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}(A\otimes B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}A\otimes^{\prime}f^{*}B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}(A\otimes^{\prime}B)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m^{\prime}}

is commutative. Unwinding the definitions, we obtain the more explicit diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{T}^{*}pr^{12,*}_{1}f^{*}A\otimes\Delta_{T}^{*}pr^{12,*}_{2}f^{*}B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{T}^{*}(pr^{12,*}_{1}f^{*}A\otimes pr^{12,*}_{2}f^{*}B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}A\otimes f^{*}B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{T}^{*}((f\times f)^{*}pr^{12,*}_{1}A\otimes(f\times f)^{*}pr^{12,*}_{2}B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{T}^{*}(f\times f)^{*}(pr^{12,*}_{1}A\otimes pr^{12,*}_{2}B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}(A\otimes B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}(\Delta_{S}^{*}pr^{12,*}_{1}A\otimes\Delta_{S}^{*}pr^{12,*}_{2}B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}(\Delta_{S}^{*}(pr^{12,*}_{1}A\otimes pr^{12,*}_{2}B))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}

that we decompose as

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{T}^{*}(f\times f)^{*}pr^{12,*}_{1}A\otimes\Delta_{T}^{*}(f\times f)^{*}pr^{12,*}_{2}B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}\Delta_{S}^{*}pr^{12,*}_{1}A\otimes f^{*}\Delta_{S}^{*}pr^{12,*}_{2}B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
